It then sheds load, the shed requests are retried, and it stays overloaded after the cause is gone, until load is shed or capacity is added.\"\n\nSo once a service is overloaded, it STAYS overloaded due to retries, even after the original cause is gone. To break the cycle, I need to either:\n- Add capacity (scale) to give headroom\n- Throttle to shed load without retries (throttle rejects up front, NOT retried)\n- Wait for retries to drain\n\nThe key insight: `throttle` rejects requests up front and they are NOT retried. This can break a retry storm! If I throttle an overloaded service's callers, the retries stop, and the overloaded service can recover.\n\nBut actually, throttle is applied to a service to make IT reject requests. Hmm, let me re-read: \"throttle(service, percent) makes the service reject that share of requests (0 to 90) up front, from the next minute. Callers see rejected requests fail, but they are not retried.\"\n\nSo if I throttle a service, it rejects some of its OWN incoming requests, and those rejections are NOT retried by callers. This reduces load on the service and breaks retry storms.\n\nBut wait, the overload is on import-svc and identity-svc. Their callers are retrying. If I throttle the CALLERS, they'd reject... no.
